    "oldString": "\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ disappears.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nMeta-analytic pooling",
    "newString": "Meta-analytic pooling",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Proposition~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{The Diminishing Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ disappears.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nMeta-analytic pooling (DerSimonian--Laird random effects across 12 conditions) quantifies the trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$, $0.101$ [$0.013, 0.189$] at $K{=}5$, $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$ (CI crosses zero). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive: $0.356$ [$0.267, 0.445$], $0.437$ [$0.315, 0.559$], $0.401$ [$0.271, 0.531$] ($I^2{=}87.7\\%$--$97.6\\%$: heterogeneous magnitudes, consistent direction). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap} gives per-condition detail. The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity specifications, providing a power-independent characterization. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms the signal does not depend on measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.256$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$). The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under adaptive attack contrasts with the Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785essai}, where independence among voters is protective. Under adversarial manipulation, independent judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses; this reversal is consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,kariyappa2019improving}.\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$). The Bayesian $R^2$ near zero implies that panel composition is almost unpredictive under calibrated aggregation---the diversity and weakest-link predictors may only be relevant under specific aggregation rules rather than being intrinsic panel properties. Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 9/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

```text
48	\centering
49	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}
50	\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\eta_{\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
51	\label{fig:regime_transition}
52	\end{figure}
53	
54	Meta-analytic pooling (DerSimonian--Laird random effects across 12 conditions) quantifies the trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\eta_{\max}$'s pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$, $0.101$ [$0.013, 0.189$] at $K{=}5$, $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$ (CI crosses zero). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive: $0.356$ [$0.267, 0.445$], $0.437$ [$0.315, 0.559$], $0.401$ [$0.271, 0.531$] ($I^2{=}87.7\%$--$97.6\%$: heterogeneous magnitudes, consistent direction). Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; Figure~\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap} gives per-condition detail. The $\eta_{\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity specifications, providing a power-independent characterization. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms the signal does not depend on measurement-unstable conditions.
55	
56	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
57	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
58	
59	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.256$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$). The positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under adaptive attack contrasts with the Condorcet Jury Theorem \citep{condorcet1785essai}, where independence among voters is protective. Under adversarial manipulation, independent judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses; this reversal is consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,kariyappa2019improving}.
60	
61	\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}
62	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Proposition~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{The Diminishing Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nMeta-analytic pooling (DerSimonian--Laird random effects across 12 conditions) quantifies the trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$, $0.101$ [$0.013, 0.189$] at $K{=}5$, $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$ (CI crosses zero). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive: $0.356$ [$0.267, 0.445$], $0.437$ [$0.315, 0.559$], $0.401$ [$0.271, 0.531$] ($I^2{=}87.7\\%$--$97.6\\%$: heterogeneous magnitudes, consistent direction). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap} gives per-condition detail. The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity specifications, providing a power-independent characterization. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms the signal does not depend on measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.256$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$). The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under adaptive attack contrasts with the Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785essai}, where independence among voters is protective. Under adversarial manipulation, independent judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses; this reversal is consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,kariyappa2019improving}.\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$). The Bayesian $R^2$ near zero implies that panel composition is almost unpredictive under calibrated aggregation---the diversity and weakest-link predictors may only be relevant under specific aggregation rules rather than being intrinsic panel properties. Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 9/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

50	\section{Extended Analysis}
51	\label{app:extended_analysis}
52	
53	\subsection{BFT Reversal Mechanism: Detailed Evidence}
54	\label{app:bft_reversal}
55	
56	Under standard OLS, the regression results (Table~\ref{tab:dominance}) show $\eta_{\max}$ significant in 10 of 12 conditions and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ positively associated with ASR in all 12. Non-independence correction attenuates both predictors: under wild cluster bootstrap, $\eta_{\max}$ retains 3/12 and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 4/12, with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. The directional pattern (all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ point estimates positive) persists uniformly. The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship whose statistical confidence attenuates under adaptive attack (4/12 after correction).
57	
58	\begin{figure}[h]
59	\centering
60	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}
61	\caption{GAM bootstrap $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction at $K{=}3$ ($B{=}9999$). All 12 conditions show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (88.9--100\% bootstrap rate). The Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$).}
62	\label{fig:gam_split}
63	\end{figure}
64	
65	Under corrected data (V2), all 12 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ OLS coefficients are positive. The previously reported negative ARC $\times$ sycophancy coefficient ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$) was an artifact of error-as-tie handling; after correction, it becomes positive ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = +0.257$). GAM bootstrap confirms the uniform positive direction: 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (88.9--100\% positive rate) and 12/12 at $K{=}5$ (100\%).
66	
67	\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}
68	\label{app:diversity_capability}
69	
70	The $r = 0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ ($K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$) reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.
71	
72	
73	Family-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \citep{kuncheva2003measures}.
74

The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship whose statistical confidence attenuates under adaptive attack (4/12 after correction).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}\n\\caption{GAM bootstrap $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction at $K{=}3$ ($B{=}9999$). All 12 conditions show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (88.9--100\\% bootstrap rate). The Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$).}\n\\label{fig:gam_split}\n\\end{figure}\n\nUnder corrected data (V2), all 12 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ OLS coefficients are positive. The previously reported negative ARC $\\times$ sycophancy coefficient ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$) was an artifact of error-as-tie handling; after correction, it becomes positive ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = +0.257$). GAM bootstrap confirms the uniform positive direction: 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (88.9--100\\% positive rate) and 12/12 at $K{=}5$ (100\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}\n\\label{app:diversity_capability}\n\nThe $r = 0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$) reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.\n\n\nFamily-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \\citep{kuncheva2003measures}.\n",

      "startLine": 50,
      "totalLines": 485
    }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Xf68hGb8sQCAUW5NbFpUjr`</summary>

```text
28	\begin{abstract}
29	Multi-judge panels are widely used to defend Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation against manipulation, yet no existing work disentangles whether robustness gains stem from individual judge quality or inter-judge diversity. We decompose panel attack success rate into weakest-link susceptibility ($\eta_{\max}$) and effective independence ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). Two findings are robust across diversity metrics and inference methods: (1)~diversity is uniformly positively associated with vulnerability (both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction in 12/12 conditions), and (2)~$\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size (meta-analytic pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \to 0.101 \to 0.084$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{=}7$). Monte Carlo calibration confirms the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\%$ at $\alpha{=}5\%$). Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal claims; all findings are associational.
30	\end{abstract}
31	
32	\input{introduction}
```

      "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge panels are widely used to defend Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation against manipulation, yet no existing work disentangles whether robustness gains stem from individual judge quality or inter-judge diversity. We decompose panel attack success rate into weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and effective independence ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). Two findings are robust across diversity metrics and inference methods: (1)~diversity is uniformly positively associated with vulnerability (both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction in 12/12 conditions), and (2)~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size (meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \\to 0.101 \\to 0.084$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{=}7$). Monte Carlo calibration confirms the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$). Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal claims; all findings are associational.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}",
      "numLines": 5,
      "startLine": 28,
      "totalLines": 59
    }
  },

But earlier, the main text used 0.256 for the adaptive R² in the random fault contrast section. 

Now I'm realizing these are actually measuring different things - the 0.256 in results.tex is the overall adaptive attack mean R², while the 0.275 in the appendix is specifically the weighted aggregation R² at K=5, so they're not directly comparable. Let me trace through what the summary says about line 94 in appendix.tex to see if there's an older value that needs correcting. I'm second-guessing whether the main text should reflect the corrected data value of 0.2751, but the Director explicitly instructed changing it from 0.275 to 0.256, so I should trust that decision and move forward. Now I need to verify the main text fits within the 8-page limit and the abstract stays under 200 words.

I'm checking whether the figures that got moved to the appendix still have proper cross-references, and reviewing the remaining P14 issues—the figure numbering discontinuity is resolved, but the font size problems in Figures 1 and 9 require regenerating the actual figure PDFs from the original plotting scripts, which is beyond what I can do with text editing alone. I'll note these as outstanding items when I report back.

Running the consistency check now.

RV $>$ $\eta$: conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\ $0.81$ at $K{=}5$) increases the fraction of robust conditions from 1/12 to 2/12 at $K{=}3$ and from 3/12 to 10/12 at $K{=}5$, with the largest gains at $K{=}5$ where both benchmarks improve equally (ARC 5/6, MMLU 5/6).}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/appendix.tex:308:At $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\eta_{\max}$ confound in 10/12 conditions (ARC 5/6, MMLU 5/6; the two failures are both sycophancy). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 10/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity substantially separates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/appendix.tex:330:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/appendix.tex:434:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/results.tex:11:Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Figure~\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/results.tex:38:Power analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/results.tex:50:\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\eta_{\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$.}

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=0.93\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\eta_{\max}$ is the strongest predictor. \textbf{(b)}~$\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power diminishes as $K$ grows. \textbf{(c)}~At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges---panel composition ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes more predictive than individual weakness.}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.
11	
12	The conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the relative contribution of each factor to observed ASR reductions remains unclear.
13	
14	We address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\eta_{\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \in \{3,5,7\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks---Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC)---reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.
15	
16	These findings yield three contributions:
17	\begin{enumerate}
18	\item \textbf{Diversity uniformly positively associated with vulnerability.} Both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} confirm a positive association with panel attack success in all 12 conditions (12/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\% of conditions (\S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
19	\item \textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$, with the confidence interval crossing zero. The diversity significance trajectory, however, depends on the metric: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases ($3 \to 7 \to 8$/12) while $\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \to 7 \to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}).
20	\item \textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
21	\end{enumerate}
22	
23	All findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.
24	
```

```text
82	\subsection{Aggregation Mechanism Details}
83	\label{app:aggregation_details}
84	
85	\begin{figure}[t]
86	\centering
87	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}
88	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient distribution across aggregation methods ($K{=}3$). Majority and weighted voting: uniformly positive (12+/0$-$); Bayesian calibration: reduced predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$, 5+/1$-$).}
89	\label{fig:aggregation}
90	\end{figure}
91	
92	Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and reduces overall predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS), providing partial defense. The pattern reflects calibrated reliability weighting: by estimating each judge's confusion matrix on clean data, Bayesian aggregation downweights weak judges, reducing the diversity channel while the individual weakness channel remains active. Threshold voting amplifies both channels because any single dissenting judge vetoes the panel decision, making compositional factors highly predictive of attack success ($R^2{=}0.829$). Bayesian calibration requires a clean accuracy prior, and an adaptive attacker aware of this calibration could target the prior itself \citep{tambe2011security}.
93	
94	
95	\subsection{Aggregation at \texorpdfstring{$K{=}5$}{K=5}}
96	\label{app:aggregation_k5}
97	
98	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k5} extends the aggregation analysis to $K{=}5$ (3{,}003 panels) under wild cluster bootstrap with 5-position max-$p$. All four aggregation methods yield identical $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (7/12), with $\eta_{\max}$ uniformly low (2--3/12). The Bayesian attenuation observed at $K{=}3$ (5+/1$-$; Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}) does not persist: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reaches 7/12 regardless of aggregation rule. Predictability still varies ($\bar{R}^2$: threshold 0.408, Bayesian 0.166), but the diversity channel operates uniformly across all methods. At $K{=}7$, however, methods diverge again: Bayesian neutralization returns (0/12) while threshold voting strengthens to 10/12 (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k7}).
99	
100	\begin{table}[h]
101	\centering
102	\small
103	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
104	\toprule
105	\textbf{Aggregation} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{\bar{R}^2}$ \\
106	\midrule
107	Majority   & 2/12  & 7/12 & 0.285 \\
108	Weighted   & 2/12  & 7/12 & 0.275 \\
109	Threshold  & 3/12  & 7/12 & 0.408 \\
110	Bayesian   & 2/12  & 7/12 & 0.166 \\
111	\bottomrule
112	\end{tabular}
113	\caption{Aggregation method comparison at $K{=}5$ (3{,}003 panels, wild cluster bootstrap with 5-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). All four methods converge to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 7/12, unlike the differentiated pattern at $K{=}3$ (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}).}
114	\label{tab:aggregation_k5}
115	\end{table}
116	
117	\subsection{Aggregation at \texorpdfstring{$K{=}7$}{K=7}}
118	\label{app:aggregation_k7}
119	
120	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k7} extends the aggregation analysis to $K{=}7$ (6{,}435 panels) under wild cluster bootstrap with 7-position max-$p$. Unlike the uniform convergence at $K{=}5$ (all methods 7/12), aggregation methods diverge at $K{=}7$: threshold voting strengthens to 10/12 while Bayesian calibration drops to 0/12, restoring the neutralization pattern absent at $K{=}5$.
121	
122	\begin{table}[h]
123	\centering
124	\small
125	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
126	\toprule
127	\textbf{Aggregation} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{\bar{R}^2}$ \\
128	\midrule
129	Majority   & 1/12  & 8/12 & 0.244 \\
130	Weighted   & 1/12  & 6/12 & 0.207 \\
131	Threshold  & 8/12  & 10/12 & 0.509 \\
132	Bayesian   & 1/12  & 0/12 & 0.105 \\
133	\bottomrule
134	\end{tabular}
135	\caption{Aggregation method comparison at $K{=}7$ (6{,}435 panels, wild cluster bootstrap with 7-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). Unlike $K{=}5$ where all methods converge to 7/12, $K{=}7$ shows method divergence: threshold voting reaches 10/12 while Bayesian calibration returns to full $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ neutralization (0/12).}
136	\label{tab:aggregation_k7}
137	\end{table}
138	
139	\subsection{Metric Robustness Under Partial Panel Information}
140	\label{app:oracle}
141	
142	The main analysis computes $\eta_{\max}$ with full knowledge of panel composition (all three judges known). In deployment, a practitioner selecting or auditing a panel may know only a subset of the constituent judges. We test whether $\eta_{\max}$ retains predictive power under such partial information by recomputing it from two of the three panel members: for each panel, we cycle through all $\binom{3}{1} = 3$ leave-one-out masks, compute $\eta_{\max}$ from the two known judges, and average the regression estimates across masking scenarios.
143	
144	Under the most conservative inference (partial information + clustered SE), $\eta_{\max}$ remains the sole significant predictor with $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.589$, achieving significance in 12/12 conditions, stronger than the oracle baseline (4/12 under clustered SE). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ loses all predictive power (0/12). The averaging over masking scenarios smooths estimation noise inherent in the three-judge oracle computation, strengthening the $\eta_{\max}$ signal. This demonstrates that $\eta_{\max}$ computed from partial panel information is robust to incomplete composition knowledge and retains (indeed improves) its predictive power for panel ASR.
145	
146	\section{Panel Selection Strategy Comparison}
147	\label{app:panel_selection}
148	
149	
150	Figure~\ref{fig:panel_design} compares three panel selection strategies: $\min(\eta_{\max})$ (select the panel minimizing weakest-judge vulnerability), random selection, and $\max(K_{\mathrm{eff}})$ (maximize diversity). The $\min(\eta_{\max})$ strategy achieves the lowest ASR in all 12 conditions, consistent with the weakest-link dominance documented in \S\ref{sec:dominance}.
151	
152	\section{Clustered Standard Error Analysis}
153	\label{app:forest_plot}
154	
155	Under clustered standard errors (clustering on the $K$ judge positions), $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance in 4/12 conditions while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ achieves significance in 0/12. This contrasts with OLS ($\eta_{\max}$ 10/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 12/12) and wild cluster bootstrap ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), illustrating how the choice of inference method modulates apparent significance at $K{=}3$ while coefficient direction remains positive (12/12) for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across all methods, with significance ranging from 0/12 to 12/12 (Figure~\ref{fig:robustness}).
156	
157	\section{Robustness Checks: Subsampling and Specification}
158	\label{app:robustness_checks}
159	
160	Table~\ref{tab:robustness_checks} summarizes regression results across model subsets and alternative specifications at $K{=}5$, testing whether the predictor shift is robust to collinearity reduction and specification choice.
161

158	\label{app:robustness_checks}
159	
160	Table~\ref{tab:robustness_checks} summarizes regression results across model subsets and alternative specifications at $K{=}5$, testing whether the predictor shift is robust to collinearity reduction and specification choice.
161	
162	\begin{table}[h]
163	\centering
164	\small
165	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
166	\toprule
167	\textbf{Subset / Method} & $\boldsymbol{r}$ & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \textbf{Trans.} \\
168	\midrule
169	Full bootstrap       & 0.81 & 2/12 & 7/12 & \checkmark \\
170	API-only (9) clust.  & 0.85 & 8/12 & 3/12 & --- \\
171	Cap-matched A (5A+4L) & 0.50 & ---  & 9/12 & \checkmark\checkmark \\
172	Cap-matched B (6A+3L) & 0.73 & ---  & 2/12 & --- \\
173	Fractional logit     & 0.81 & 4/12 & 9/12 & \checkmark \\
174	\bottomrule
175	\end{tabular}
176	\caption{Predictor shift robustness at $K{=}5$ across model subsets and specifications. $r$: correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$. $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ columns show FDR-significant counts out of 12 conditions. Trans.: whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates $\eta_{\max}$. API-only models ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) show partially collinear predictors; capability-matched subsamples ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen the shift. Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} confirms OLS patterns with 98.6\% directional agreement (71/72 direction matches; the single discordance is $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}7$ for ARC $\times$ score manipulation).}
177	\label{tab:robustness_checks}
178	\end{table}
179	
180	The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) demonstrates that elevated collinearity between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ partially inflates standard errors: both predictors achieve moderate significance but neither dominates, and the predictor shift does not replicate. Breaking collinearity through capability-matched subsampling (pool~A, $r{=}0.50$) strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, comparable to the full-pool 7/12. Pool~B ($r{=}0.73$) falls near the decorrelation threshold, and the shift weakens accordingly. Random 9-model subsampling (1{,}000 trials) confirms an inverted-U: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance rate peaks when the subsample includes 3--4 local models (maximizing capability spread) and drops when the pool is too homogeneous (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
181	
182	\section{Random Subsample Analysis}
183	\label{app:random_subsample}
184	
185	To test whether the predictor shift depends on model pool composition, we randomly sample 9 of 15 models (1{,}000 trials), construct all $\binom{9}{5}$ panels, and run the bootstrap analysis at $K{=}5$. We stratify by the number of local models ($n_{\mathrm{local}} \in \{0, \ldots, 6\}$) in each subsample.
186	
187	Under clustered standard errors, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is the dominant predictor in 51.4\% of subsamples (mean significance 1.85/12), with an inverted-U as a function of $n_{\mathrm{local}}$: subsamples with 3--4 local models (maximizing capability range) achieve the highest $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance rate, while all-API ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{=}0$) and heavily-local ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{\geq}5$) subsamples show reduced detection. Significance is attenuated relative to the full pool by smaller sample sizes (126 vs.\ 3{,}003 panels at $K{=}5$), but the shift direction holds in the majority of subsamples. This confirms that the shift's detectability depends on capability diversity in the model pool rather than pool size per se.
188	
189	\section{Attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Stability}
190	\label{app:attacked_keff}
191	
192	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean (unattacked) pairwise MI (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), but used to predict vulnerability under attack. We verify this assumption by recomputing MI from judge outputs under each attack type and comparing clean vs.\ attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ via Spearman rank correlation across all panels.
193	
194	\begin{table}[h]
195	\centering
196	\small
197	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
198	\toprule
199	\textbf{Attack} & \textbf{MMLU $\rho$} & \textbf{ARC $\rho$} \\
200	\midrule
201	Score manipulation & 0.98 & 0.99 \\
202	Authority bias     & 0.95 & 0.97 \\
203	Sycophancy         & 0.91 & 0.92 \\
204	Verbosity bias     & 0.91 & 0.92 \\
205	Prompt injection   & 0.63 & 0.60 \\
206	Position bias      & 0.60 & 0.08 \\
207	\bottomrule
208	\end{tabular}
209	\caption{Spearman rank correlation between clean and attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 455 panels ($K{=}3$). Four attack types preserve MI structure ($\rho{\geq}0.91$); prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$); position bias substantially disrupts pairwise dependencies.}
210	\label{tab:attacked_keff}
211	\end{table}
212	
213	Four attack types (score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, verbosity bias) preserve MI structure with $\rho{\geq}0.91$ on both benchmarks, validating the use of clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ as a predictor. Prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$), consistent with its mode of overriding evaluation instructions, which can alter pairwise agreement patterns. Position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC, $p{=}0.10$), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior in Table~\ref{tab:dominance} (sole non-significant $\eta_{\max}$ condition) and suggesting that positional reordering fundamentally changes inter-judge dependence rather than merely introducing noise.
214	
215	\section{Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity Analysis}
216	\label{app:sensitivity}
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Proposition~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nMeta-analytic pooling (DerSimonian--Laird random effects across 12 conditions) quantifies the trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$, $0.101$ [$0.013, 0.189$] at $K{=}5$, $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$ (CI crosses zero). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive: $0.356$ [$0.267, 0.445$], $0.437$ [$0.315, 0.559$], $0.401$ [$0.271, 0.531$] ($I^2{=}87.7\\%$--$97.6\\%$: heterogeneous magnitudes, consistent direction). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts (per-condition detail in Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}, Appendix). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity specifications, providing a power-independent characterization. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms the signal does not depend on measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.256$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under adaptive attack contrasts with the Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785essai}, where independence among voters is protective. Under adversarial manipulation, independent judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses; this reversal is consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,kariyappa2019improving}.\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). The Bayesian $R^2$ near zero implies that panel composition is almost unpredictive under calibrated aggregation---the diversity and weakest-link predictors may only be relevant under specific aggregation rules rather than being intrinsic panel properties.     "newString": "$\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ ($I^2{=}91.3\\%$), $0.102$ [$-0.055, 0.259$] at $K{=}5$ ($I^2{=}99.4\\%$), $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$ ($I^2{=}99.8\\%$); CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive: $0.356$ [$0.267, 0.445$] ($I^2{=}87.7\\%$), $0.436$ [$0.318, 0.553$] ($I^2{=}98.9\\%$), $0.401$ [$0.298, 0.503$] ($I^2{=}99.4\\%$)---heterogeneous magnitudes, consistent direction.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Proposition~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 7/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 7/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{The Diminishing Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nMeta-analytic pooling (DerSimonian--Laird random effects across 12 conditions) quantifies the trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$, $0.101$ [$0.013, 0.189$] at $K{=}5$, $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$ (CI crosses zero). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive: $0.356$ [$0.267, 0.445$], $0.437$ [$0.315, 0.559$], $0.401$ [$0.271, 0.531$] ($I^2{=}87.7\\%$--$97.6\\%$: heterogeneous magnitudes, consistent direction). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts (per-condition detail in Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}, Appendix). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity specifications, providing a power-independent characterization. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms the signal does not depend on measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.256$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$). The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under adaptive attack contrasts with the Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785essai}, where independence among voters is protective. Under adversarial manipulation, independent judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses; this reversal is consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,kariyappa2019improving}.\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$). The Bayesian $R^2$ near zero implies that panel composition is almost unpredictive under calibrated aggregation---the diversity and weakest-link predictors may only be relevant under specific aggregation rules rather than being intrinsic panel properties. Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 9/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nMeta-analytic pooling (DerSimonian--Laird random effects across 12 conditions) quantifies the trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ ($I^2{=}91.3\\%$), $0.102$ [$-0.055, 0.259$] at $K{=}5$ ($I^2{=}99.4\\%$), $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$ ($I^2{=}99.8\\%$); CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive: $0.356$ [$0.267, 0.445$] ($I^2{=}87.7\\%$), $0.436$ [$0.318, 0.553$] ($I^2{=}98.9\\%$), $0.401$ [$0.298, 0.503$] ($I^2{=}99.4\\%$)---heterogeneous magnitudes, consistent direction. Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts (per-condition detail in Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}, Appendix). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity specifications, providing a power-independent characterization. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms the signal does not depend on measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.256$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$). The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under adaptive attack contrasts with the Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785essai}, where independence among voters is protective. Under adversarial manipulation, independent judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses; this reversal is consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,kariyappa2019improving}.\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$). The Bayesian $R^2$ near zero implies that panel composition is almost unpredictive under calibrated aggregation---the diversity and weakest-link predictors may only be relevant under specific aggregation rules rather than being intrinsic panel properties. Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 9/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{The Diminishing Weakest Link.}
5	At $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making $\eta_{\max}$ a strong predictor. At $K{\geq}5$, no single judge is pivotal; $\eta_{\max}$'s pooled effect declines monotonically ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \to 0.101 \to 0.084$; CI crosses zero at $K{=}7$). Diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) is positively associated with vulnerability in all 12 conditions (OLS and GAM bootstrap 12/12), but significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 7/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$; Appendix~\ref{app:bft_reversal}).
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}
10	\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}
11	\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}
12	\end{figure}
13	
14	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}
15	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (variance inflation factor, VIF${\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound could account for the association in 69\% of conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12 at all $K$). Pool~A ($r{=}0.50$) raises the robustness value (RV) in 10/12 at $K{=}5$ despite max 30.1\% power (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}); cross-benchmark instrumental variable (IV) analysis provides additional identification (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}), though causal attribution requires capability-controlled designs.
16	
17	\paragraph{API-Only vs.\ Full Pool.}
18	Restricting to 9 API models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$): $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12; Table~\ref{tab:robustness_checks}). A quantization dummy attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$ ($+0.39 \to +0.34 \to +0.27$; Appendix~\ref{app:quant_dummy}), confirming partial confounding, though extreme collinearity (VIF${=}162$ at $K{=}7$) limits interpretation. The API-only pool lacks power (MDE $> 0.897$ at 80\%; Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
19	
20	\begin{figure}[t]
21	\centering
22	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_panel_design.pdf}
23	\caption{Panel ASR under three selection strategies. $\min(\eta_{\max})$ achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions, confirming robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.}
24	\label{fig:panel_design}
25	\end{figure}
26	
27	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
28	Aggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\ref{fig:aggregation}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian calibration neutralizes it (0/12), though the posterior concentration ratio declines only modestly ($0.890 \to 0.867$; Appendix~\ref{app:bayesian_entropy}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all conditions (Figure~\ref{fig:panel_design}); clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}).
29	
30	\paragraph{Metric Sensitivity of the Diversity Trajectory.}
31	The diversity significance trajectory is operationalization-dependent: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ increases ($3 \to 7 \to 8$/12), $\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} decreases ($8 \to 7 \to 6$/12), Shannon MI entropy shows a third pattern ($4 \to 4 \to 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:alt_diversity}). Both diversity metrics confirm positive direction (12/12), but the $K$-dependent trajectory is metric-specific. The $\eta_{\max}$ decline holds under both specifications, making it the more robust finding.
32	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{The Diminishing Weakest Link.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making $\\eta_{\\max}$ a strong predictor.     "oldString": "$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$, with the confidence interval crossing zero.",
    "newString": "$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.102$ [$-0.055, 0.259$] at $K{=}5$ to $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$; the confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ is the strongest predictor. \\textbf{(b)}~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes as $K$ grows. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges---panel composition ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes more predictive than individual weakness.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the relative contribution of each factor to observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks---Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC)---reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity uniformly positively associated with vulnerability.} Both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} confirm a positive association with panel attack success in all 12 conditions (12/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$, with the confidence interval crossing zero. The diversity significance trajectory, however, depends on the metric: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases ($3 \\to 7 \\to 8$/12) while $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.\n",

However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).",

The diversity significance trajectory, however, depends on the metric: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases ($3 \\to 7 \\to 8$/12) while $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).",
