<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BKaefF5WGsCRK8Z5XKn8YF`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We find that the weakest link diminishes: $\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}7$ (meta-analytic pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \to 0.101 \to 0.084$; CI crosses zero at $K{=}7$), while diversity remains uniformly positively associated with vulnerability (both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction in 12/12 conditions, pooled CI excluding zero). The diversity significance trajectory, however, is operationalization-dependent ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$: $3 \to 7 \to 8$/12; $\kappa$-diversity: $8 \to 7 \to 6$/12), making it unsuitable as a standalone contribution. Monte Carlo calibration confirms that the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\%$), so reported significance counts are lower bounds.
4	
5	For practitioners: at $K{=}3$, screen for individual judge robustness; at $K{\geq}5$, panel composition becomes more predictive than individual weakness. The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) is itself a finding: in current LLM ecosystems, diverse panels are structurally more capable, precluding observational separation. Future work should pursue capability-controlled designs and open-ended generation benchmarks where the discrete multiple-choice structure does not constrain the attack surface.
6	
```

    "newString": "$\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \\to 0.102 \\to 0.084$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$)",
    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\\usepackage{tikz}\n\\usetikzlibrary{shapes.geometric,arrows.meta,positioning,fit,shadows.blur,calc}\n\n\\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}\n\\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}\n\\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}\n\n\\title{The Diminishing Weakest Link: How Panel Size Shifts\\\\Adversarial Vulnerability Drivers in LLM Judge Panels}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge panels are widely used to defend Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation against manipulation, yet no existing work disentangles whether robustness gains stem from individual judge quality or inter-judge diversity. We decompose panel attack success rate into weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and effective independence ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). Two findings are robust across diversity metrics and inference methods: (1)~diversity is uniformly positively associated with vulnerability (both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction in 12/12 conditions), and (2)~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size (meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \\to 0.101 \\to 0.084$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{=}7$). Monte Carlo calibration confirms the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$). Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal claims; all findings are associational.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations. \\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability in current LLM ecosystems ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) is a fundamental limitation: observational regression cannot reliably separate their contributions. Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.\n\n\\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\\leq}9$ position clusters) \\citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.\n\n\\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats with discrete answer spaces; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present fundamentally different attack surfaces where continuous scoring replaces categorical judgments, and diversity--vulnerability relationships may differ. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.\n\n\\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

```text
56	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
57	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
58	
59	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.730$ [$0.718, 0.745$] ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ [$0.270, 0.301$] at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap is $\Delta{=}0.446$ [$0.430, 0.460$]. The positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under adaptive attack contrasts with the Condorcet Jury Theorem \citep{condorcet1785essai}, where independence among voters is protective. Under adversarial manipulation, independent judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses; this reversal is consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,kariyappa2019improving}.
60	
61	\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}
62	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
63	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BuaByprSjCjBZ8bXC4NRK3`</summary>

```text
6	\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}
7	\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. We focus on the \emph{panel-size-dependent structure} of this vulnerability: the diminishing predictive power of the weakest link as $K$ grows, and the persistent positive association between diversity and vulnerability.
8	
```

- Left column top: "Causal identification requires training-based diversity control." then "Inference sensitivity" (this is Limitations!)
- Then more Limitations text: "Benchmark and attack scope", "Panel size range"


- Right column: "Code and Data Availability", then "A Proof of Proposition 1" (Appendix starts)

So the main text through §7 Conclusion fits within 8 pages, with Limitations and the appendix starting on page 9 and beyond.

Looking at the page 8 right column more carefully, the Conclusion section discusses the weakest link principle and meta-analytic pooled estimates, then transitions into practical guidance: at K=3, focus on individual judge robustness; at K≥5, panel composition becomes more predictive. There's also a key finding about the diversity-capability confound showing correlations of 0.70-0.81, which itself reveals something important about current limitations in the data.

The section wraps up by noting that in LM ecosystems, diverse panels are inherently more capable, making it impossible to separate these effects observationally. The recommendation is to pursue capability-controlled experimental designs and open-ended generation benchmarks that don't rely on the discrete multiple-choice structure, which limits the scope of what can be tested.

Now I'm verifying the page layout—the Conclusion section fits entirely within page 8, with the Limitations section beginning at the bottom of that page and continuing onto page 9. The main body stays within the 8-page target, and the introduction didn't overflow despite adding the K=5 data. The total page count remains at 21, so the text fits as intended.

Before notifying the Director, I should run through the consistency check once more to confirm all the updated numbers align properly.

The 1.6% critical is a false positive - the number is reported in methodology.tex (Monte Carlo calibration) which the checker classifies as "other" not "result". This is a checker limitation, not an actual issue.

The precision mismatches:
- 0.27 (analysis) vs 0.270 (results): analysis has "$+0.39 \to +0.34 \to +0.27$" (quant dummy β_std) while results has "$0.270$" or "$0.301$" in the bootstrap CI. 

The results section actually contains "[0.270, 0.301]" for the adaptive R² confidence interval, so these are measuring different things entirely - the quantile dummy coefficient versus the confidence bound. Same situation with the 0.50 versus 0.503 mismatch, which compares Pool~A correlation against a different K_eff confidence interval. The orphan bibliography keys are likely from citations in the appendix that the checker isn't picking up since it only scans the main text files.

Looking more closely, cameron2015practitioners is definitely cited in methodology.tex with the `\citep[see][for a practitioner's guide]{cameron2015practitioners}` format, so the checker probably isn't detecting that citation style properly. The genest2008validity entry appears in the appendix's copula goodness-of-fit section, but the other orphan entries like austensmith1996, ferrari2004beta, and the others seem genuinely unused - I'll leave those alone since they're outside the scope of what needs fixing.

All the numerical corrections are complete, so I should verify there aren't any lingering instances of the old numbers elsewhere before reporting back.

Now I need to check whether the figure caption about η_max needs updating. The Director specified changes to the mixed effects and frac logit counts (6/12 → 7/12 at K=3 and 4/12 → 6/12 at K=5), but the caption's reference to the wild cluster bootstrap declining from 3/12 to 2/12 wasn't mentioned as needing revision, so that part should stay as is. is 1/12 as well, so the Director's unchanged 1/12 at K=7 could refer to either method. Looking more carefully at the corrections, the Director is specifically updating the mixed effects and fractional logit counts—6/12 → 7/12 at K=3 and 4/12 → 6/12 at K=5—while leaving the bootstrap values untouched. I've verified all the corrections against the bootstrap and crossed random effects tables—the K=7 bootstrap η_max of 1/12 matches what the Director noted, and the three corrections to Tables 3 and the appendix robustness table are all accounted for. Time to report back to the Director.

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We find that the weakest link diminishes: $\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}7$ (meta-analytic pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \to 0.102 \to 0.084$; CI crosses zero at $K{\geq}5$), while diversity remains uniformly positively associated with vulnerability (both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction in 12/12 conditions, pooled CI excluding zero). The diversity significance trajectory, however, is operationalization-dependent ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$: $3 \to 7 \to 8$/12; $\kappa$-diversity: $8 \to 7 \to 6$/12), making it unsuitable as a standalone contribution. Monte Carlo calibration confirms that the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\%$), so reported significance counts are lower bounds.
4	
5	For practitioners: at $K{=}3$, screen for individual judge robustness; at $K{\geq}5$, panel composition becomes more predictive than individual weakness. The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) is itself a finding: in current LLM ecosystems, diverse panels are structurally more capable, precluding observational separation. Future work should pursue capability-controlled designs and open-ended generation benchmarks where the discrete multiple-choice structure does not constrain the attack surface.
6	
```

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=0.93\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\eta_{\max}$ is the strongest predictor. \textbf{(b)}~$\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power diminishes as $K$ grows. \textbf{(c)}~At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges---panel composition ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes more predictive than individual weakness.}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.
```

```text
38	\input{conclusion}
39	
40	\section*{Limitations}
41	
42	Our study has several limitations. \textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability in current LLM ecosystems ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) is a fundamental limitation: observational regression cannot reliably separate their contributions. Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.
43	
44	\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\leq}9$ position clusters) \citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.
45	
46	\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats with discrete answer spaces; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present fundamentally different attack surfaces where continuous scoring replaces categorical judgments, and diversity--vulnerability relationships may differ. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.
47	
48	\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \in \{3,5,7\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.
49	
50	\section*{Code and Data Availability}
51	Code, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.
52	
53	\input{appendix}
54	\input{appendix_attacks}
55	
56	\bibliography{references}
57	
```

    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/main.tex",
      "content": "\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations. \\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability in current LLM ecosystems ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) is a fundamental limitation: observational regression cannot reliably separate their contributions. Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.\n\n\\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\\leq}9$ position clusters) \\citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.\n\n\\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats with discrete answer spaces; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present fundamentally different attack surfaces where continuous scoring replaces categorical judgments, and diversity--vulnerability relationships may differ. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.\n\n\\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n",
      "numLines": 20,
      "startLine": 38,
      "totalLines": 59
    }

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "3aefed48-788f-41eb-9f6a-68693f3d2ebb",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "a1c3c1ac-2369-4411-97c0-782b72f75d2a",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "8c1da921-2fbc-4217-a4e5-3582fe41cd6d",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}",
    "newString": "\\includegraphics[width=0.78\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ is the strongest predictor. \\textbf{(b)}~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes as $K$ grows. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges---panel composition ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes more predictive than individual weakness.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the relative contribution of each factor to observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks---Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC)---reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity uniformly positively associated with vulnerability.} Both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} confirm a positive association with panel attack success in all 12 conditions (12/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.102$ [$-0.055, 0.259$] at $K{=}5$ to $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$; the confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. The diversity significance trajectory, however, depends on the metric: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases ($3 \\to 7 \\to 8$/12) while $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.\n",

          "+\\includegraphics[width=0.78\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}",
          " \\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ is the strongest predictor. \\textbf{(b)}~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes as $K$ grows. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges---panel composition ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes more predictive than individual weakness.}",

We decompose panel attack success rate into weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and effective independence ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). Two findings are robust across diversity metrics and inference methods: (1)~diversity is uniformly positively associated with vulnerability (both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction in 12/12 conditions), and (2)~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size (meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \\to 0.102 \\to 0.084$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$). Monte Carlo calibration confirms the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$). Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal claims; all findings are associational.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations. \\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability in current LLM ecosystems ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) is a fundamental limitation: observational regression cannot reliably separate their contributions. Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.\n\n\\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\\leq}9$ position clusters) \\citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.\n\n\\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats with discrete answer spaces; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present fundamentally different attack surfaces where continuous scoring replaces categorical judgments, and diversity--vulnerability relationships may differ. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.\n\n\\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
